(COARSE and FINE). Such adjustment at                   visual meter and recorder will vary.
each low-reference signal may be needed if a

should then be made quickly so that a suitable

trace can be recorded during the time between

the completion of the adjustment and the termi-

censional rate is high, the recorded low-refer-

ence traces may be too short to provide a

usable record both before and after the adjust-

ment. In such instances no adjustment should

be made because the low-reference record is

usually so scattered that there is doubt as to

the actual value of the low-reference frequen-

cies of the traces.  .

41. Transmission Line
                                                          | LOWER LEG
   a. A coaxial cable is used for the trans-              l      (SKIRT)
mission line. This cable is composed of a seven-
strand copper wire inside of, and surrounded              |
coaxially by, a tubular, copper shielding braid,          |
or outside conductor (see par. 6f). In this
line, the electromagnetic field is mostly con-            | |
fined to the space between the inner and outer
conductors; and hence, there is little loss of             |              CONDUCTOR
power from the line due to radiation or induc-             |r*— OUTER     CONDUCTOR
tion. The outside surface of the outer line also
serves as a Shield to exclude unwanted waves                  |
originating from other sources.
                                                          | |
    b. The characteristic impedance of the line
is determined from the ratio of the diameter              | Fagecx |NNER
of the inner conductor to the inside diameter of
the outer conductor. The characteristic imped-            iF|
ance in the case of the particular cable in use
is 52+8 ohms. If a coaxial line is terminated             Mirae  - OHM    COAXIAL               CABLE
by an impedance equal to the characteristic
impedance then the load presents no reflection            i!
to the incoming signal. A constant impedance

    b. OSCILLOSCOPE CIRCUIT. Part of the output
of the first audio amplifier is fed to the ver-
tical amplifier of the oscilloscope. This signal
is also used to synchronize the time base gen-
erator. The output of the time base generator
goes to the horizontal amplifier, and thus per-
mits the oscilloscope to show a picture of the
a-f signal output of the receiver. A separate
power supply is furnished only for the oscillo-
scope.

   c. AUDIO MONITOR CIRCUIT. Part of the out-
put of the receiver is fed to a loudspeaker
amplifier and loudspeaker. This permits aural
monitoring of the output signal of the receiver.

   d. POWER SUPPLY. A full-wave rectifier stage,
suitably filtered, supplies the power for all the
circuits of the receiver, except for the oscillo-
scope which has its own power supply. A volt-
age regulator regulates the voltage supplied to
the local oscillator stage.

43. R-f Amplifier Circuits

          (fig. 23)

   The r-f unit of the radiosonde receiver con-
sists of a two stage r-f amplifier, a local oscil-

lator and a mixer. The entire unit, mounted on

a subchassis, is placed in a completely shielded
compartment to provide sufficient shielding.
The tubes may be reached by removal of the
top cover of the shield compartment.

                                            CHAPTER 5

                          FIELD MAINTENANCE INSTRUCTIONS

      Note. This chapter contains information for field maintenance. The amount of repair that can be performed
by units having field maintenace responsibility is limited only by the tools and test equipment available, and by
the skill of the repairman.

                Section |. TROUBLE SHOOTING AT FIELD MAINTENANCE LEVEL

         Warning: When servicing the equipment, be extremely careful; high voltage is present. Al-
- ways turn off the power before making resistance checks in the equipment. Keep one hand in your

  pocket when measuring socket voltages. Before touching any part of the equipment after the
  voltage is shut off, short the part to ground.

71. Trouble-Shooting Procedures                                      In order for this information to be

75. Trouble Shooting Voltage Regulator                                                           DISCHARGE CAPACITOR BY SHORTING
     CN-2A/FMQ-|                                                                                 BEFORE TOUCHING.

   Voltage Regulator CN-2A/FMQ-1 seldom                                                                                  VOLTAGE REGULATOR
fails in service. If, however, it is necessary or                                                                          CONTINUITY CHECK
desirable to check its operation, use the fol-
lowing procedures:                                                                                                                                       TM

   a. The output voltage of the regulator should
be 115 volts when the input voltage is between
95 and 180 volts at a frequency of 60 cps (fig.
49). If the voltage regulator fails to produce
a satisfactory output, check the input voltage.
It should be between 105 and 125 volts at a
frequency of 60 cps +2 cps.

    b. If the input voltage and frequency are
normal, disconnect the unit from the power
source and check the transformer windings for
open or short circuits (fig. 51).

92. Over-all Performance and Sensitivity Tests

   The receiver is said to function correctly
when an output signal of correct waveform and
magnitude, for a specified input, is obtained at
the output terminals.

   a. Connect Signal Generator TS—497/URR,
to the antenna input jack, J-101, and chassis.
Adjust the generator for an output of 2 micro-
volts or greater at 400 mc, modulated 30 per-
cent at 400 cps.

   b. Connect Electronic Multimeter TS—505/U
across the OUTPUT terminal panel, TB101
(connect the negative lead to the terminal
marked G). Set the voltmeter on its 40-volt,
a-c scale.

   c. Adjust the receiver controls as follows:
       (1) Set the POWER ON-OFF switch ON.
       (2) Set the RF GAIN LOW-HIGH switch
              at HIGH.

   CHAPTER 6

SHIPMENT AND LIMITED STORAGE AND DEMOLITION

|  TO PREVENT ENEMY USE

   Section I. SHIPMENT AND LIMITED STORAGE

93. General                                        available and the conditions under which the
                                                   equipment is to be shipped or stored. Refer to
   The circumstances involved in shipment and      paragraphs 7 and 9 and follow in reverse order
storage vary, and therefore no definite proce-     the instructions given.
dure can be given that applies in all situations.
The following instructions are intended as a          b. Whenever practicable, place a dehydrating
guide for preparing the radiosonde receptor        agent such as silica gel inside the packing. Pro-
for shipment and storage.                          tect the equipment with a waterproof paper
                                                   barrier. Seal the seams of the paper barrier with
94. Repacking for Shipment or                      a waterproof sealing compound or tape. Pack
     Limited Storage                               the protected equipment in a padded wooden
                                                   case, providing at least 3 inches of excelsior
   a. The procedure in repacking for shipment      padding or some similar material between the
or limited storage depends on the material         paper barrier and the packing case.

) Section Il. DEMOLITION OF MATERIEL TO PREVENT ENEMY USE

95. General                                            b. Cut. Cut cords, and wiring, using axes,
                                                   handaxes, or machetes.
   The demolition procedures outlined in para-
graph 96 will be used to prevent the enemy            c. Burn. Burn cords, resistors, capacitors,
from using or salvaging this equipment. Demo-      coils, wiring, and technical manuals, using
lition of the equipment will be accomplished       gasoline, kerosene, oil, flame throwers, or in-
only upon order of the commander.                  cendiary grenades.

96. Methods of Destruction                            d. Bend. Bend panels, cabinets, and chassis.

   a. Smash. Smash the controls, tubes, coils,        e. Explosives. If explosives are necessary,
switches, capacitors, transformers, loudspeak-     use firearms, grenades, or TNT.
er, and printing mechanism, using sledges,
axes, handaxes, pickaxes, hammers, crowbars,          f. Disposal. Bury or scatter the destroyed
or heavy tools.                                    parts in slit trenches, fox holes, or other holes,
                                                   or throw them into streams.

                                                      g. Destroy. Destroy everything.
